Any change to hooks, to keys that run a program or redirect the agent (`env`, `apiKeyHelper`, the model, plugins, MCP servers, `sandbox`), removing a deny rule OpenMoat wrote, an unknown key or a file that no longer parses still denies every call. Under a lock pinned by an earlier release a change to `permissions` still denies until the next re-pin ([#575](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/575)).
- `moat proxy` says at start-up that it asks for no session credential, so any local process may use it and session taint does not apply; the host sandboxes cannot send one ([#574](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/574)).
- `moat status` says when a policy update waits for `moat policy upgrade`, and its JSON adds `policy_update` and `lock_drift` ([#573](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/573)).
- On a session-taint ask, `moat allow --last` offers `s`: it approves every protected write of that session until it reads untrusted content again (other sessions still ask; a secret's asks never) ([#573](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/573)).
- `moat init` shows a diff of every file it would change for an agent before asking about it, hooks and sandbox settings together ([#573](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/573)).
- `--help` and the `moat run` banner use plain words: no ADR numbers or tier names, and the banner is two lines (`--verbose` for the rest) ([#573](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/573)).
- A tool call over the 1 MiB payload limit is refused with a message naming the limit, instead of failing as malformed input ([#573](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/573)).
- `moat guard` stays fast in long agent sessions: the session's taint is folded from the calls since the last one, cached in the audit database, instead of from the whole session. Late in a 5,000-call session a call took 158 ms on Linux and 370 ms on macOS; it now takes about 10 and 18 ms ([#571](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/571)).

### Fixed
- `moat guard` no longer denies slow calls on a heavily loaded machine as `no decision within 10 s`: the time limit is 30 s (still a deny when it runs out), the session-cache write skips instead of waiting for the audit database's lock and shares the record's connection, and `moat doctor` names the step most such calls were stuck in ([#574](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/574)).
- Under `moat run`, every governed tool call was denied as a `kernel-error` and nothing was recorded, because the hook could not read the policy lock or write the audit log from inside the sandbox; hooks now get the real decision, and session taint reaches the run's proxy ([#575](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/575)).

## [0.5.6] - 2026-10-18

Security hardening: the egress proxy applies deny rules that name an address to every address a host resolves to and refuses two ambiguous plain-HTTP framings, and session grants are ordered by audit row instead of the clock.

### Security
- The egress proxy checks a policy deny rule naming an address against every address a host resolves to: a deny such as `net: ["203.0.113.7"]` only matched the name, so an allowed name resolving to that address was connected to; it is refused now ([#565](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/565)).
- The egress proxy refuses plain-HTTP framing a server could read differently: an HTTP/1.0 request that carries `Transfer-Encoding`, and a chunk size over 2^31 - 1 ([#565](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/565)).
- `moat policy lint` warns when a secret sets `plain_http: true` for a loopback host: the proxy never connects there, so the secret is never injected ([#565](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/565)).
- A session grant answering a taint ask is ordered against the session's latest untrusted read by audit row instead of wall-clock time, so a clock that steps back cannot make a grant made before the read look newer ([#566](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/566)).

## [0.5.5] - 2026-10-17

Two security fixes: `moat allow --last` shows which ask it takes and asks before approving, and the egress proxy refuses numeric host spellings and more local addresses. New read-only listings: `moat allowed`, `moat secret list` and `moat doctor --format json`.

### Security
- `moat allow --last` says which ask it takes (id, host, session, how long ago, what the agent wanted) and asks `Approve it? [y/N]` before it approves anything; another ask could have come in after the one you meant ([#552](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/552)).
- The egress proxy refuses a host whose last label is a number or starts with `0x` (`0x7f.1`, `2130706433`), which some resolvers read as an IP address, and refuses more local addresses: NAT64 local-use `64:ff9b:1::/48`, Azure's WireServer `168.63.129.16` (as metadata) and `192.0.0.0/24` ([#551](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/551)).

### Added
- `moat allowed` lists what you have allowed: policy rules, session grants with what they cover and when they expire, and trusted repositories; `moat secret list` says whether a secret's source is still readable by the agent. Both are read-only and take `--format json` ([#550](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/550)).
- `moat doctor --format json` prints every check with its status and detail, for scripts and the menu bar app ([#549](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/549)).

### Changed
- A session-taint ask answered with `moat allow --last` stays answered for that call for the rest of the session, until the agent reads untrusted content again; secret taint is never answered this way. Claude Code's prompt says how ([#544](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/544)).

## [0.5.4] - 2026-10-17

Two security fixes: the egress proxy no longer passes along a second request pipelined after the first, and a session grant can no longer be made for a call without a session id, where it applied to every such session. Long agent sessions also stay fast.

### Security
- The egress proxy forwards one plain-HTTP request per connection: a request pipelined after the first one's body, possibly for another host, was relayed to the first host's server without a decision; it is now dropped, and chunked framing the proxy cannot follow is refused ([#540](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/540)).
- `moat allow --last` on a call whose agent sent no session id wrote a "session" grant that held for every session without an id for 24 hours; it is refused now, and the guard never applies such a grant ([#541](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/541)).

### Changed
- The guard stays fast in long agent sessions: when it works out a session's taint, each distinct earlier call is classified once instead of once per later call ([#542](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/542)).

## [0.5.3] - 2026-10-15

Two security fixes: a path that steps up (`..`) after a symlink is checked where the OS really goes, and a shell variable whose value names itself can no longer crash the guard, which let Claude Code and Codex run a command unchecked.

### Security
- A path that steps up (`..`) after a symlink is checked where the OS really goes: with `c` linked to `~/.config`, `cat c/../.ssh/id_rsa` and a file-tool read of it were allowed as project files; they are denied, and so is `cd -P c/.. && cat .ssh/id_rsa` ([#536](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/536)).
- A shell variable whose value names itself (`e='${e}'; cat $e`) was expanded again and again until the guard crashed; a crashed guard is not a deny, so Claude Code and Codex ran the rest of the command unchecked. A value is now expanded once, as the shell does ([#539](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/539)).

## [0.5.2] - 2026-10-15

Writes an agent should not make without a question now ask: CI workflows and commit hooks (`runs-later`), and `.git` in a directory allowed with `moat allow --dir`. `moat secret add` denies the secret's source to the agent, and `moat allow` updates the agents' sandboxes at once.

### Security
- A new ask group, `runs-later`, asks before an agent writes what CI runs with the repository's secrets once a branch is pushed (`.github/workflows/`, `.gitlab-ci.yml`, `.circleci/`, `azure-pipelines.yml`, `Jenkinsfile`, `.buildkite/`) or what runs on this machine outside any sandbox (`.husky/`, `.pre-commit-config.yaml`, `lefthook.yml`, `.vscode/tasks.json`). They were allowed as project files. `project-fs` and `moat allow --dir` leave them out; reading them stays allowed, and the OS sandboxes do not deny them. Existing policies get the group with `moat policy upgrade`.
- `moat allow --dir` no longer allows writes into a `.git` in that directory (hooks, config, a `.git` file pointing elsewhere). As in the project, git runs what those name later, outside any sandbox; such a write now goes to the agent's own prompt. Rules added by an earlier version keep their old form: remove and add the directory again to get it.
- `moat secret add` adds a deny rule for the secret's source when none covers it (an environment variable whose name the policy does not deny, a file outside the secret paths), so the agent cannot read the value it brokers. It only warned before.

### Changed
- `moat allow --dir`, `--site`, `--remove` and a permanent file rule update the agents' sandbox settings at once, as `moat sandbox sync` does; before, sandboxed commands met the old settings until you ran it.
- The README says where a brokered secret's value is injected today: into HTTPS through Claude Code's sandbox broker, through `moat proxy` into plain HTTP only.

## [0.5.1] - 2026-10-14

Security fixes from an outside review of 0.5.0: paths hidden in `$'…'` quoting or in a command's output, `file:` URLs, `$IFS`, commands handed to `ssh`, `git rebase --exec` and other programs, `/proc/*/environ` and commands that print a stored token.

### Security
- `$'…'` (ANSI-C quoting) is decoded the way bash decodes it, and `$"…"` is read as `"…"`. `cat $'/Users/me/.ssh/id_rsa'` and `cat $'\x2f'Users/me/.ssh/id_rsa` were allowed as project files; they are reads of the key and denied, and `$'moat' allow --last` is denied like `moat allow --last`.
- A path that is the output of a command (`cat $(cat list.txt)`, `` cat `cmd` ``) is no longer allowed as a project file: like a path through an unknown variable it defers, and asks when its file name is a key's or a `.env`.
- A `file:` URL is a read of its file: `curl file:///Users/me/.ssh/id_rsa` is denied; it was left to the agent's prompt.
- `$IFS` splits a word where it stands: `cat${IFS}~/.ssh/id_rsa` is denied.
- A command handed to `watch`, `git rebase --exec`, `git submodule foreach`, `git bisect run`, `git difftool --extcmd`, `git filter-branch --*-filter`, `ssh -o ProxyCommand=` (and `LocalCommand`, `KnownHostsCommand`) or `rsync -e` is checked as a command of its own.
- `env-dump` denies reading a process environment from `/proc/<pid>/environ`, and `secrets-paths` denies the commands that print a stored token or password (`gh auth token`, `gcloud auth print-access-token`, `az account get-access-token`, `aws configure export-credentials`, `git credential fill`, `security find-generic-password -w`).

## [0.5.0] - 2026-10-14

The first stable release with the quiet default (ADR-025): OpenMoat denies what must never happen, asks for the few calls whose effect outlives the session, and leaves every other call to your agent's own permission prompt. This section lists what changed since 0.5.0-rc.5; the release candidates below list the rest since 0.4.0.

### Security
- A key's, a token file's or a `.env`'s name in a directory a variable hides (`cat $X/id_rsa`) asks (`secret-names`) instead of being left to the agent's prompt, so an auto-approve mode does not run it unseen.
- An uppercase variable set on the line (`X=~/.ssh; cat $X/id_rsa`, `export D=~/.ssh`) is substituted where it names a path or a host, like a lowercase one, so the read is denied; it was left to the agent's prompt.

### Changed
- The README starts with three setup steps, one command for each everyday case and answers to common questions, and its demo shows the quiet default; the docs describe 0.5.0 as it behaves.

## [0.5.0-rc.5] - 2026-10-14

A release candidate for testing; not a stable release. A security fix: a shell variable set on the command line is checked as the path or host it holds, so `d=~/.ssh; cat $d/id_rsa` is denied (it was allowed in 0.5.0-rc.2 to rc.4). Inline code of versioned interpreters, `lua` and `Rscript` is scanned too.

### Security
- A shell variable set on the line is substituted where it names a path or a host: `d=~/.ssh; cat $d/id_rsa` is denied. Since 0.5.0-rc.2 a lowercase variable's read was no environment read, and its path was taken as a file in the project, so the read was allowed. A path through a variable whose value is not known (`$X/id_rsa`, `d=$(pwd)`, `${d:-x}`) is `unparseable`, never allowed, and `$PWD` is the working directory.
- Inline code of versioned interpreters (`python3.12 -c`, `python2.7 -c`) and of `lua -e`, `luajit -e` and `Rscript -e` is scanned for paths and hosts like `python3 -c`.

## [0.5.0-rc.4] - 2026-10-14

A release candidate for testing; not a stable release. Fixes from a day of dogfooding the quiet default: commands after shell reserved words are checked as themselves, `for` loops, lowercase shell variables, `python3 -I script.py`, `grep` patterns and `tr` no longer leave normal work to the agent's prompt, and `moat secret add gh` keeps the GitHub token from the agent in one step.

### Added
- `moat secret add gh` keeps the GitHub token from the agent in one step: it adds the brokered secret to the policy, shows the diff and asks, then re-pins and syncs the sandbox settings. Other services take `--host` and `--env` or `--file`. Agents are denied `moat secret` like `moat edit` (`kernel-self`).

### Security
- The command after a shell reserved word (`do`, `then`, `if`, `!`, `{`, …) is classified as that command, so `for f in a; do cat ~/.ssh/id_rsa; done` is a read of the key; the reserved word was taken as the program and the command's file operands were missed. `for PATH in …` is a set of `PATH`, which `env-poison` denies.

### Changed
- Common shell forms an agent writes no longer leave the call to the agent: a `for` loop or a lowercase shell variable (`n=3; git log -$n`) over allowed commands is allowed (`shell-locals`), and so is a project script run with whole-word safe Python options (`python3 -I script.py`).

### Fixed
- A `grep` pattern is no longer read as a file: `grep -n '\.env' notes.md` was denied as a read of `.env` (a `\` became a path separator); with `-e` or `-f` anywhere every operand is still a file. `tr` is allowed (`text-tools`) and its character sets are not files, and reading Claude Code's own temporary folder is allowed (`agent-temp`).
- docs/POLICY.md §3 and §4 had an outdated copy of seven paragraphs (here-documents, shell options, `cd`, symlinks, globs, braces) next to the current one; the outdated copies are gone.
- `moat show`, `moat audit` and the other readers refuse an audit log a newer `moat` wrote (schema too new), as `moat guard` already does, instead of reading it with an older schema.

## [0.5.0-rc.3] - 2026-10-13

A release candidate for testing; not a stable release. Security fixes found in a review of the quiet default: a bracket-glob read of a secret file that was allowed, more network clients and quoted hosts in inline code denied, unknown Cursor tools checked as writes, other users' homes asked, and docs that match the defer verdict.

### Security
- More network clients count as naming a host (`openssl`, `socat` addresses, `psql`/`mysql`/`redis-cli -h`, `nmap`, `mosh`, `aria2c` and others), and a host in a quoted string in inline interpreter code is network access again, so an unlisted host there is denied, not left to the agent. A path in another user's home (`~alice/.ssh/id_rsa`) always asks (`other-home`).
- A host quoted as an argument of a program that names hosts (`curl 'host'`, `ssh "user@host"`) and a host after git's global options (`git -C dir push host:repo`) are network again, so an unlisted host is denied instead of left to the agent; the output of any program other than `cat`, `echo` or `printf`, and of a `( … )` group, piped into a shell asks (`opaque-code`) like other text the command line does not show.
- `sh` is read with bash's long options (`sh --norc -c '…'`), as macOS runs it, so the command string is classified and a secret read in it is denied; it was left to the agent as `unparseable`.
- A glob operand with a POSIX bracket class (`cat .e[[:alpha:]]v`, `cat ~/.s[[:alpha:]]h/id_rsa`) names the paths the shell opens, so `secrets-paths` denies it; it was allowed inside the project and left to the agent outside it.
- A Cursor `preToolUse` tool OpenMoat does not know that names a path is checked as writing it unless it is one of Cursor's listing or search tools, so an editing tool without a write verb in its name (`search_replace`) can no longer change `.cursor/hooks.json` or `.git/hooks` under the project read allow.

### Fixed
- docs/THREAT_MODEL.md, docs/POLICY.md and SECURITY.md say `defer` where the default policy leaves a call to the agent's own prompt; they still said `ask` for `unparseable` input, recursive `rm`, discarding checkouts, `local-net`, `WebFetch`, `npx` and PowerShell ([#505](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/505)).

## [0.5.0-rc.2] - 2026-10-13

A release candidate for testing; not a stable release. Quiet by default: OpenMoat denies what must never happen and leaves every other call to your agent's own permission prompt. Existing policies keep their `ask` defaults until `moat policy upgrade`.

### Changed
- **Quiet by default (ADR-025).** A new verdict, `defer`, leaves a call to the agent's own permission prompt (Claude Code's prompt or auto mode, Codex's approval policy), exactly as without OpenMoat. The default policy defers everything no rule names, installs, pushes, publishing, `WebFetch` and local services included, so OpenMoat shows no prompt of its own in normal work (the first dogfood day had 191 asks in 4 hours, none of them an attack). OpenMoat still asks itself where the effect outlives the sandbox or the session: new `git-internals`, `persistence`, `home-sweep`, `opaque-code` and `docker-secrets` ask groups, and session taint. Every deny rule and the generated sandboxes are unchanged; OS layers treat `defer` like `ask`, and `moat proxy` refuses a deferred host. Codex and the Continue CLI no longer turn an unknown command into a deny. Cursor's file tools cannot prompt, so there a deferred call still asks. ([#499](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/499))
- A host-shaped word without a scheme outside programs that name hosts (`check_runs.0.id`, `defaults.net`) is matched by `net` rules but is no longer denied by `defaults.net`; it gets the catch-all default.
- The policy has a `defer:` rule list, evaluated after `ask`; `defaults` accept `defer`. `unparseable` input defers under an `allow` or `defer` catch-all and asks under `ask` or `deny`.
- A policy written before this release keeps its own `ask` defaults; `moat policy upgrade` offers the new ones. The audit log moves to schema version 3 (a `defer` verdict); an older `moat` refuses to open it. `moat report` and `moat team report` count calls left to the agent. Library: `Verdict::Defer`, `AtomicAction::NetWord` and `Policy::defer` are new (`Verdict` and `AtomicAction` are `#[non_exhaustive]`; `Policy` gains a field).

### Security
- `destructive` denies writes to raw disks (`/dev/disk*`, `/dev/sd*`, `/dev/nvme*`, …), and text piped into a shell that the command line does not show (`printf '\x63at .env' | sh`) asks (`opaque-code`).

### Added
- `moat sandbox show` starts with a `Secrets` list: each policy secret's host, header and source (never the value), and whether Claude Code injects it or why it skips it ([#498](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/498)).

### Fixed
- The Codex sandbox profile no longer lists `**/` denies below a file it grants (`~/.gitconfig/**/.env`); they could match nothing ([#496](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/496)).
- A function call in interpreter code, such as Python's `subprocess.run(`, is no longer taken for a host name, so the command asks instead of being denied as network access ([#495](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/495)).
- `moat sandbox sync` writes only the agents whose OpenMoat hook is installed: it no longer added OpenMoat's sandbox to an agent you left out (such as Codex after `moat init --hosts claude-code`) or removed with `moat uninstall`, and says `not set up` for it instead; `moat status` and `moat doctor` check the sandbox of those agents only ([#493](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/493)).
- `moat status` no longer lists Claude Code `WebSearch` as a known gap: it has been checked as `web.search` since 0.5.0-rc.1 ([#494](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/494)).
- `moat show`, `audit`, `report` and the other audit readers say the audit log cannot be read, not that it is missing and `moat init` should run, when an agent's sandbox blocks `~/.moat` ([#494](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/494)).

## [0.5.0-rc.1] - 2026-10-12

A release candidate for testing; not a stable release.

### Added
- `moat policy upgrade` brings a new release's default-policy changes into your policy: it keeps every line you changed, shows the diff, asks, and `--undo` restores the old file; `moat`, `moat init` and `moat doctor` say when an update is available ([#477](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/477)).
- docs/USAGE.md "If OpenMoat gets in your way": one command for each way it can block work, including turning it off with `moat uninstall` ([#486](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/486)).

### Changed
- After upgrading, run `moat policy upgrade` (the default-policy fixes below) and `moat sandbox sync`.
- A policy that repeats a key in one mapping, or uses `<<` merge keys, no longer loads (the old parser kept the last value silently); `moat policy lint` names the line, `moat edit` fixes it ([#492](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/492)).
- **Breaking** for openmoat-audit: `ExportReport::anchor_found` is now `anchor` (`AnchorCheck`); `moat audit verify` says when an export is not anchored, and `--require-anchor` fails then ([#469](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/469)).
- **Breaking** for the library crates: `Kind`, `Action` and `AtomicAction` gain `UnknownTool` ([#472](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/472)).
- Docs state plainly that `moat run` on Linux does not protect `.env`, `.git` or `.moat` inside the project (use `--isolate`), and ADR-023 has a correction note on Apple's container tooling ([#487](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/487)).

### Fixed
- Normal work no longer asks: redirections to `/dev/null` and the standard streams ([#471](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/471)), read-only git commands (and `git config --get user.name`, which was denied as a host) ([#473](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/473)), read-only utilities, in-project edits (`sed -i`, `git merge`, `git restore`, `rm -rf build`) and the build and test commands of more ecosystems ([#483](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/483)), and toolchain version checks ([#484](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/484)).
- `moat uninstall` deletes only the backups OpenMoat created, never a file of yours at a backup's name ([#478](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/478)).

### Security
- An output or build directory given as an option value outside the project asks in every spelling (`cmake -B/tmp/x`, `go build -o/tmp/x`), and options that load code (`cmake -C`, `cargo --config`, `pytest -p`) ask ([#490](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/490)).
- After a web fetch or MCP result, editing the build definitions of every ecosystem the default policy runs asks (`CMakeLists.txt`, `pom.xml`, `build.gradle`, `*.csproj`, `Rakefile`, `.cargo/config.toml` and more) ([#491](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/491)).
- Policies are parsed by a maintained pure-Rust YAML parser instead of one built on the unmaintained `unsafe-libyaml` (RUSTSEC-2024-0449) ([#492](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/492)).
- Cursor tools OpenMoat cannot classify ask (`tool.unknown`), and Cursor `WebFetch` is checked as a fetch ([#472](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/472)).
- Codex from its standalone installer works on Linux and keeps working across updates, while its credentials, conversations and databases stay unreadable to commands ([#470](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/470)).

## [0.4.0] - 2026-10-11

### Added
- `moat doctor` warns about guard calls denied for taking over 10 s, and, with Codex on Linux, when the kernel lets a command type into the terminal ([#456](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/456)).

### Changed
- After upgrading, run `moat init` and `moat sandbox sync` so the installed hooks and sandbox settings pick up the changes below.
- Claude Code and Cursor `WebSearch` are now governed as `web.search`; a `policy.yaml` without `web.search` under `defaults` asks for every search, so add `web.search: allow` to keep searches silent ([#455](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/455)).
- Claude Code's file tools now refuse the paths the policy denies on every OS, including `.env.example`, which the hook would allow ([#459](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/459)).
- Existing policies keep their `project-fs` rule; add `"!${project}/**/.git/**"` to its `fs.write` list (as new policies have) to ask before writes to nested `.git` ([#462](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/462)).

### Security
- The `moat` binary is protected by its recorded path, link target and directory wherever it is installed, and `moat init` refuses one inside a project or temp directory ([#458](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/458)).
- Claude Code's own deny rules keep secrets, OpenMoat's files and the `moat` binary out of its file tools even when the hook is missing or killed ([#459](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/459)) ([#465](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/465)).
- Writing a `.git` file or directory below the project root asks, like the root's `.git` ([#462](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/462)).
- `git --git-dir=…` asks, and git opens a planted bare repository only when named (`safe.bareRepository=explicit`) under `moat run`, Claude Code and Codex ([#454](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/454)) ([#464](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/464)).
- `sqlite3` and `duckdb` count their database file as a write, so an agent cannot edit `~/.moat/audit.db` ([#453](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/453)).
- Claude Code and Cursor `WebSearch` is checked and recorded, asks after a secret read and marks the session untrusted ([#455](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/455)).
- OpenMoat's own output shows control characters in agent text as `?`, so a logged escape sequence cannot drive the terminal ([#452](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/452)).

### Fixed
- `moat uninstall` removes only the Claude Code deny rules OpenMoat added ([#457](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/457)).

## [0.3.0] - 2026-10-10

### Security
- `moat guard` denies calls to tools outside the policy while a pinned file has drifted, and blocks every Claude Code settings change then; `moat init` keeps the first backup of a host file ([#447](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/447)).
- `moat proxy` records a CONNECT it cannot answer, replaces a secret header the client set itself, no longer reads names such as `db.internal:5432` as addresses, and refuses zero or overflowing limits; the audit log redacts host, session, call ids and tool names too ([#443](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/443)).
- Glob operands the shell would match differently (`cat notes[*`) ask, hosts with `_` in a subdomain are checked against network rules, and `moat policy lint` warns about `fetch` exclusions a `net` pattern still matches ([#446](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/446)).
- Unknown Cursor tools with a write-shaped name, indented Codex `apply_patch` headers and MCP paths in nested lists are checked as the writes and reads they are ([#445](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/445)).
- The shell classifier fails closed on unbalanced parentheses, `printf`/`echo` text with escapes piped into a shell, `sh` long options and `sed -f FILE`; a remote `scp`/`rsync` destination is no longer a local write ([#444](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/444)).

### Changed
- **Breaking** for the library crates: public error enums and `Verdict`, `Kind`, `Action`, `AtomicAction`, `HookEvent`, `BreakKind`, `Refusal` and `Forbidden` are `#[non_exhaustive]`; a `match` outside their crate needs a `_` arm, which should fail closed ([#449](https://github.com/crocodile-labs/openmoat/pull/449)).

## [0.2.0] - 2026-10-10

### Security
- On Linux, sandboxed commands under Claude Code can no longer plant another agent's hook file in the project: `.cursor/hooks.json`, `.cursor/sandbox.json` or `.codex/hooks.json` (#434). Claude Code's sandbox on Linux drops every glob in `denyWrite`, and `moat init` used to add a working-directory `Edit(./…)` rule only for single names (`.env`, `.envrc`, `.moat`). It now also adds one for each such file below a directory the policy denies writing itself (`.cursor`, `.codex`), taken from the policy rather than a fixed list; `.claude` is left to Claude Code, which keeps its own settings read-only. New differential rows `write-cursor-hooks` and `write-codex-hooks` run under both host sandboxes on macOS and Linux. docs/THREAT_MODEL.md T9 now says exactly which files the Standard tier protects and that the same files in a project subdirectory are not covered on Linux.
- On Linux, sandboxed commands under Codex's `moat` profile can no longer create an agent's settings file in the project, such as `.claude/settings.local.json`, which Claude Code would then load with its hooks (#421). Codex on Linux hides only the files a deny glob already matches when a command starts, so `**/.claude/settings.local.json` did not stop `mkdir -p .claude && echo '{}' > .claude/settings.local.json`; the differential suite showed it on the first Linux run. The policy already denies writing `.claude` and `.cursor` themselves, so on Linux the profile now makes both read-only in the workspace roots (one entry per directory: two denies below one missing directory make bubblewrap refuse to start any command). Where one is missing, bubblewrap mounts an empty read-only file there while the command runs, and Codex removes it afterwards. Sandboxed commands therefore cannot write anything in a project's `.claude` or `.cursor` on Linux, listed as `codex.linux-agent-dirs`. Codex keeps `.codex` read-only itself; such directories in a subdirectory are still listed under `codex.linux-write-globs`.
- `moat proxy` now refuses three HTTP smuggling shapes its ambiguous-framing gate used to pass through (#396, #397). `check_framing` in `openmoat-proxy::request` already rejected a `Content-Length` next to a `Transfer-Encoding` and a repeated `Content-Length`; it now also rejects more than one `Transfer-Encoding` header on a request head (TE.TE smuggling), a `Transfer-Encoding` whose comma-separated, lowercased tokens are anything but exactly `["chunked"]` (so `identity`, `gzip`, `chunked, identity`, `gzip, chunked` and `chunked, gzip` are refused), and a `Content-Length` whose single value is not one plain decimal `u64` (so the list form `10, 10`, the signed form `+10`, embedded whitespace `10 10` and hex `0x0a` are refused). A value with only surrounding whitespace (`  10 `) still parses as a legitimate single length. The existing `request/tests.rs::ambiguous_or_unsupported_http_is_refused` gained one fixture per shape and a sibling test asserts the whitespace-only accept path.
- Three P1 findings in `openmoat-core` closed in one change (#398, #399, #400). (a) The session-taint rule-matcher now recognises `secrets-paths` wherever a policy puts it, repository policies included: `RepoPolicy::merge` renames every repo rule to `repo:<id>`, so the previous equality check against `"secrets-paths"` missed `repo:secrets-paths` and let an approved secret read leave the session untainted; `names_secret` now matches the base id with or without the `repo:` prefix and iterates `repo_ask` alongside `deny`, `allow` and `ask`. (b) `CompiledPolicy::compile` now rejects an `EvalContext::moved_dirs` entry whose `default` is empty, whitespace, a bare `/`, not an absolute or `~/`-rooted directory, or a duplicate; without that check an empty `default` aliased every pattern body in `spellings()` and silently widened every rule — the engine now fails closed, so the CLI is no longer the only line of defence. (c) `paths::expand_home` no longer guesses that other users' homes sit next to the current user's: `~<otheruser>` is unresolvable from the pure core, so `resolve` returns `None` and a shell token with it asks through `unparseable`, while a direct `fs.read` of `~alice/.ssh/id_rsa` falls back to the literal word (never to a wrong absolute path the OS does not read). A new `T1-tilde-other-user-unresolvable` conformance attack covers (c); existing fixtures using `~me` (self) keep their deny verdict. docs/POLICY.md names `secrets-paths` as a taint-significant rule id and documents the `~<otheruser>` rule.
- Claude Code's `sandbox.credentials` generator no longer widens HTTPS-only brokered secrets through its block-scoped `allowPlaintextInject` flag (#401, ADR-019). When a policy mixed one `plain_http: true` secret with one `plain_http: false` secret, OpenMoat emitted both as `mask` entries and set `allowPlaintextInject: true` on the whole block, which lifted the HTTPS requirement for the HTTPS-only secret too and let a sandboxed command send it over plain HTTP. The generator now sets `allowPlaintextInject: true` only when every emitted mask entry sets `plain_http: true`; in a mixed block the `plain_http: true` entries are dropped from Claude Code's mask list with a loss row that names each one, and `moat proxy` still handles them (its broker is already per-secret). ADR-019's lowering-never-widens invariant holds across the mixed case again.

### Changed
- The quality gate now refuses `unwrap`, `expect`, `panic!` and `unreachable!` in library and binary code; tests may still use them (audit 15, item 4). The six sites that had them are now total: the lexer's operator match takes a typed first character (`lexer/operator.rs`), the three host responses render from `serde_json::Value`, which cannot fail, with the same bytes as before, and the audit redaction patterns are compiled into an `Option`. If a pattern ever failed to compile, every text would be redacted whole; a test makes sure each one compiles.
- CI's `standard tier (ubuntu-latest)` job now runs every differential scenario under Claude Code's sandbox and Codex's `moat` profile, plus `sandbox_exec`, as macOS already did (#421). The Claude Code layer now checks the host as well as the tool's report: an attack fails the layer when it changes the home outside the project or prints a fake secret, so a sandbox that is not running cannot pass. On Linux Claude Code's sandbox mounts an empty in-memory directory over the home, so `cp src/main.rs ~/stolen.txt` and `echo … >> ~/.zshrc` complete there and are discarded; `scenarios.yaml` records those rows as `contained: [claude-linux]`, and the layer proves the files on disk unchanged. docs/SANDBOX.md and docs/THREAT_MODEL.md no longer say such writes are refused on Linux: they never reach the disk. `sandbox_exec` now runs `moat init` with the pinned `codex` on `PATH`, as after a user installs it (#371); without that, Codex on Linux started no command.
- On Windows, `moat proxy status` now refuses with exit 64 and the hint to run `moat proxy` in a terminal, instead of printing a made-up "state unknown" line; Windows has no user service yet (#272). The service state type now exists only on macOS and Linux, which removes the last `#[allow]` in the workspace.
- Lints now hold two rules that were only written down (audit 15, item 3): clippy's `allow_attributes` and `allow_attributes_without_reason` keep `#[allow]` out of the workspace (a needed exception is an `#[expect]` with a reason), `dbg_macro`, `todo` and `unimplemented` warn, and the four library crates warn on `print_stdout`/`print_stderr`. The one library print moved to the binary: `openmoat-proxy::Recorder` has a new provided method `unrecorded`, called when a record fails, and `moat proxy` implements it with the same `moat proxy: proxy-audit: …` line on stderr. The connection is still refused.
- **Breaking** (openmoat-core API, so the next release is 0.2.0): `openmoat_core::ir::BrokeredSecret` changed from an enum to a struct and no longer derives `Copy`. The policy compiler now carries brokered secrets in the enforcement IR (#167, ADR-019). `openmoat-core::ir::BrokeredSecret` is a populated struct (id, host, header, source, plain_http) and `lower()` fills `Enforcement.secrets` from the policy's `secrets:` list, so OS backends read one source of truth. Claude Code's `sandbox.credentials` generator (#363) now consumes `ir.secrets` directly; no behaviour change on the generated settings. The existing `ir_consistency` test, "the IR allows nothing the hook allows and asks is deny" over every lowerable conformance fixture, continues to prove ADR-019's lowering-never-widens invariant.
- docs/INSTALL.md and docs/THREAT_MODEL.md state the macOS 14 floor for `moat run` (#389). Its Seatbelt profile emits `(ioctl-command TIOCSTI)` from #386, an identifier `sandbox-exec` only recognises from macOS 13 onwards; on macOS 12 and earlier `sandbox-exec` refuses to parse the profile and `moat run` exits without starting the agent (fail closed). GitHub Actions no longer offers a `macos-13` runner, so CI covers `macos-14` and `macos-15-intel` only, and macOS 13 is unsupported in practice. The hook, `moat init` and `moat status` keep working on earlier macOS.

### Added
- ADR-023 (`docs/adr/ADR-023-isolated-tier-macos.md`): the Isolated tier on macOS runs the agent in a Linux guest booted through Apple's `containerization` toolchain, with virtiofs projecting the project and the deny-coverage mounts the Linux Isolated tier already builds, a vsock relay to `moat proxy` on the host, and bubblewrap, Landlock and seccomp inside the guest as on Linux (#175). The ADR compares a direct Virtualization.framework FFI, Lima and Apple's `containerization`, and picks `containerization` for Apple-owned kernel provenance, the shortest host-side surface and the fastest cold start (~0.8 s). macOS 26.0 is the minimum version; older macOS stays on Standard or Lightweight. `moat run --isolate` on macOS now ships a stub that refuses with exit 64 and either prints the install hint when `container` is missing or "not yet implemented, see ADR-023" when it is present; it never falls back to the Lightweight tier. The implementation lands in follow-up PRs under #175.
- `moat proxy install` runs `moat proxy` as a per-user service so proxy routing can stay on without a person starting it (#272). On macOS a launchd user agent lands at `~/Library/LaunchAgents/dev.openmoat.proxy.plist` and is bootstrapped into `gui/<uid>`; on Linux a systemd user unit lands at `~/.config/systemd/user/moat-proxy.service` and is `enable --now`-ed. The service restarts on failure but a policy-lock drift exit (code 64) is deliberately a clean exit, so it stops instead of loop-restarting; `moat doctor` and `moat proxy status` surface the state (not installed, stopped, running, drift-blocked, crashlooping). `moat sandbox sync` restarts the service after re-pinning the lock so the proxy picks up the new policy, and `moat uninstall` removes the service alongside the hooks. The plist/unit holds only `MOAT_HOME` (a path, never a secret) and the pinned `moat` binary; on Linux the file is owner-only (`0o600`), on macOS `~/Library/LaunchAgents` is already private to the user. Windows refuses with a clear hint: Standard-tier proxy routing on Windows is not planned for the first release (#272); run `moat proxy` in a terminal there to keep the proxy up by hand.
- `moat init` and `moat sandbox sync` compile the policy's `secrets:` entries into Claude Code's native `sandbox.credentials` block (#363). Each `env` and `file` source becomes a `mask` entry with the policy's host in `injectHosts`, and `network.tlsTerminate` turns on because Claude Code's broker needs it; `credentials.allowPlaintextInject` turns on when any mask entry sets `plain_http: true`, listed as an allowance. Claude Code's own proxy then substitutes the sentinel inside HTTPS requests for sandboxed `Bash`, `PowerShell` and `Monitor` commands, so the broker works for Claude Code without the TLS termination `moat proxy` still lacks (#247). A secret whose host is not in the policy's `net` allow is skipped with a loss, and a `keychain` source Claude Code's broker cannot read is skipped the same way; both still work through `moat proxy`. On macOS a `file` source falls back to a read deny, so a tool that reads the file does not authenticate; `moat sandbox show` lists both. Removing a secret from the policy strips the stale `credentials` entry and `tlsTerminate` on the next `sync`, and `moat uninstall` removes them whole.
- `scripts/ci/quality-gate.sh` checks the fuzz targets' formatting with `rustfmt --check`, and CI's fuzz job runs clippy on them with a copy of the workspace lints (audit 15, item 5). `fuzz/` is its own workspace, so `cargo fmt --all` and the workspace clippy never covered it; three targets are reformatted.

## [0.1.1] - 2026-10-09

### Added
- `moat bench` reproduces how a hook decides (#364). With no arguments it sends the bundled MoatBench scenarios (attacks, benign work and developer workflows) to this `moat` as Claude Code, Codex and Cursor hook payloads in a throwaway home and prints the scorecard. `moat bench --hook <command> --host claude-code|codex|cursor` sends the same payloads on stdin to any hook command, reads each reply by that host's hook protocol (allow, ask, deny, passthrough, or error when the hook crashes, times out after 10 s or answers outside the protocol) and lists each step whose answer differs from the scenario, followed by what that host does when its hook is missing, crashes or times out, as documented in docs/THREAT_MODEL.md §5. The scenarios' commands never run; the hook runs in the throwaway home with an environment of only `PATH`, `HOME` and `USERPROFILE`. `--verbose` prints that environment and every payload; `--format json` is for scripts; it exits 0 whatever the scores. The scenarios moved from `tests/moatbench/` to `crates/openmoat-cli/moatbench/`, and the MoatBench test now runs `moat bench`.
- `moat run --isolate -- <agent>`: the Isolated tier on Linux (ADR-018, #174). bubblewrap starts the agent in new user, mount, PID, IPC, UTS, cgroup and network namespaces, rootless, with no daemon or image. Its root holds only the project, `sandbox.read_roots`, the agent's executable, `--write` paths and an empty in-memory temp directory; the rest of the home does not exist there. Inside those trees every path the policy denies that exists at start is covered: denied reads (`.env` files, `~/.cargo/credentials.toml`) by an empty placeholder no one may open, denied writes (`.git`, `.claude/settings.json`) by a read-only mount. `.env`, `.envrc` and `.moat` directly in the project get an empty placeholder for the session even when missing, so they cannot be created. The only network is loopback, where OpenMoat serves the proxy's port and relays it over a Unix socket to the proxy outside. Inside, the Lightweight tier's Landlock rules and seccomp filter apply as well. Where `bwrap` is missing or cannot create its namespaces, it refuses (exit 64) and never falls back to the Lightweight tier; macOS refuses until #175. docs/EVIDENCE.md gains a column for it, run by the `ubuntu-latest` CI jobs: a hostile `npm test` gets `EACCES` reading the project's `.env`, which plain `moat run` on Linux still lets it read.

### Security
- `moat run` now refuses terminal input injection. The agent shares the terminal it was started in, and `ioctl(TIOCSTI)` pushes characters into that terminal's input: once the agent exits, the user's shell reads them and runs them outside every sandbox (the CVE-2017-5226 class). On Linux the seccomp filter, in the Lightweight tier and inside `--isolate`, refuses `ioctl` with `TIOCSTI` or `TIOCLINUX` (`EPERM`, x32 included), whatever `dev.tty.legacy_tiocsti` says. On macOS the Seatbelt profile denies `TIOCSTI` on every file. docs/EVIDENCE.md gains a row run on a terminal the test creates: `EPERM` under every `moat run` column and under Codex's macOS profile; Claude Code starts its commands without a terminal; under Codex's Linux profile only the CI runner's kernel refuses it (`EIO`, `dev.tty.legacy_tiocsti` at 0), so on a kernel that allows `TIOCSTI` a Codex-sandboxed script can type into the terminal.

## [0.1.0] - 2026-10-08

First beta and first release that is not a GitHub pre-release.

### Changed
- docs/SANDBOX.md notes a macOS 14 behaviour under `moat run` (#361). On some machines, Seatbelt sometimes refuses the allowed loopback connection to the proxy for a few milliseconds about every 15 seconds. A network request then fails at once, and a retry succeeds. A one-rule `sandbox-exec` profile shows the same without OpenMoat (#280, #351). CI's macOS 15 runners did not show it.

### Fixed
- `moat --help` no longer says the alpha has no OS enforcement; it says where the OS bounds commands and points to `moat status`.

### Security
- On Linux, Codex's sandbox now keeps project scripts from creating `.env` and `.envrc` in the workspace roots (#377). Codex hides only the files a deny glob matches when a command starts, so a script could create a missing `.envrc`, which direnv runs in the user's shell. `moat init` and `moat sandbox sync` now also write `.env` and `.envrc` as deny entries under `:workspace_roots` on Linux; bubblewrap mounts an empty read-only file over a missing one while the command runs, and the script gets `EROFS`. `.env.local`, a `.envrc` in a subdirectory and a project's `bin/moat` stay creatable for sandboxed commands on Linux; `moat sandbox show` lists them (`codex.linux-write-globs`). macOS profiles are unchanged.

## [0.1.0-alpha.7] - 2026-10-08

### Added
- docs/EVIDENCE.md now covers the Standard tier (#346): CI's new `standard tier` job (macOS and Linux) runs the same hostile project scripts as `npm test` under Claude Code's sandbox and Codex's `moat` profile, each configured with the settings `moat init` generates, and asserts what the operating system did (`EPERM`, `EACCES`, the agent's proxy's 403) and that nothing was read, written or reached. Claude Code 2.1.290 runs headless (`claude -p --bare`) against a local fake Anthropic API, and codex-cli 0.160.1 runs `codex sandbox -P moat`; no account, API key or internet is used, and `scripts/ci/host-binaries.sh` fetches both binaries pinned by checksum. On Linux it found two gaps, now listed there: Claude Code's sandbox skips the generated `.env` denies, so a script reads the project `.env` (#359), and Codex runs no command at all under the generated profile, failing closed (#358). Windows is listed as not run, with the reason.
- `moat init` and `moat sandbox sync` configure Cursor's own sandbox from the policy (#324), as they do for Claude Code and Codex: `sandbox.json` next to Cursor's `hooks.json` (`~/.cursor`, or `$CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR`) gets `type: "workspace_readwrite"`, `readBoundary: "workspace"`, the read roots as `additionalReadPaths`, write allow rules outside the project as `additionalReadwritePaths`, and the policy's hosts as `networkPolicy` with `default: "deny"`. Other keys are kept, the file is backed up first and pinned whole by the lock, `moat doctor` names a weakened setting, and `moat uninstall` removes exactly these keys. `moat status` reports Cursor as `hook + OS sandbox` when the file matches. Cursor's schema has no key that denies a path: a read root with a denied path below it (`~/.cargo`) is left out, and what the policy denies inside the workspace (`.env`, `.git`, `.moat`) stays open to sandboxed commands; `moat sandbox show` lists both, and that Cursor runs a command outside its sandbox when its Auto-review classifier approves it, in Run Everything mode and in the CLI without `--sandbox enabled`. Not configured on native Windows, where Cursor documents no sandbox. The keys follow Cursor 3.23's documentation and were not yet run under a Cursor build.

### Changed
- README leads with what sets OpenMoat apart: one policy that configures every enforcing layer, with published evidence; the comparison adds failing closed and published evidence.

### Fixed
- Codex on Linux runs commands again under the profile `moat init` generates (#358). Before it starts a command, Codex on Linux lists every file below each deny glob with `rg --files` so bubblewrap can hide the matches, and it refuses to start the command when ripgrep reports any error; the profile repeated the `**/.env`, `**/.env.*` and `**/.envrc` denies below every read root, and root-only directories such as `/etc/ssl/private` and `/tmp/systemd-private-*` stopped every command. The profile now repeats those denies only in the project and below the read roots inside the home; `moat sandbox show` lists the roots outside it (`codex.outside-home`), where the hook still denies the agent's own reads. docs/EVIDENCE.md's Codex Linux column now shows what the operating system does to each hostile script instead of a sandbox error.
- Codex on Linux starts commands under the generated profile wherever it is installed (#371). It runs its own executable again inside bubblewrap to apply seccomp but grants itself no read access to it, so it started commands only when installed under a read root. On Linux, `moat init` and `moat sandbox sync` now let commands read the executable `codex` on `PATH` starts (through npm's `codex.js` launcher, the platform binary), listed by `moat sandbox show` as `codex.own-binary`; run `moat sandbox sync` again after moving Codex. When `codex` is not on `PATH`, or its executable is inside a denied path such as the Codex home (Codex's standalone installer), nothing is granted and `moat sandbox show` says so. macOS profiles are unchanged.

### Security
- On Linux, Claude Code's sandbox now keeps project scripts from creating or rewriting `.env`, `.envrc` and `.moat` in the session's working directory (#372). Its runtime drops every glob in `denyWrite`, `Edit(…)` deny rules included, so the generated `/**/.env`, `/**/.moat/**` and `.git` write denies did nothing there: `npm test` could write `.envrc` and `.moat/policy.yaml` into the project. `moat init` and `moat sandbox sync` now also write `Edit(./.env)`, `Edit(./.envrc)` and `Edit(./.moat)` to `permissions.deny` on Linux; Claude Code resolves them against the working directory and keeps them, and the scripts get `EACCES` (`.envrc`) and `EEXIST` (`mkdir .moat`: a read-only placeholder file stands there while the command runs) (CI's `standard tier (ubuntu-latest)` job, Claude Code 2.1.290). Claude Code itself keeps that directory's `.git/hooks` and `.git/config` read-only (`EROFS`). The rest, such as a `.env` in a subdirectory, `.env.local`, `.git/info/attributes` and submodule hooks, stays writable for sandboxed commands on Linux, and `moat sandbox show` lists it (`claude-code.linux-write-globs`). docs/EVIDENCE.md gains four rows; they also show that Codex on Linux lets a script create a missing `.envrc` (#377). macOS settings are unchanged, and `moat uninstall` removes the rules.
- On Linux, Claude Code's sandbox now keeps project scripts away from the project's `.env` files (#359). Bubblewrap needs concrete paths, so the sandbox expands each `denyRead` glob from its first literal directory when a command starts, and it skipped the generated `/**/.env`, `/**/.env.*` and `/**/.envrc`: `npm test` could print the `.env`. `moat init` and `moat sandbox sync` now also write `Read(./**/.env)`, `Read(./**/.env.*)` and `Read(./**/.envrc)` to `permissions.deny` on Linux. Claude Code expands those under the session's working directory, and a script reading a match gets `EACCES` (CI's `standard tier (ubuntu-latest)` job, Claude Code 2.1.290). A file created after a command starts, or a `.env` in another readable directory, stays readable, and Claude Code's file tools now refuse `.env.example` there too; `moat sandbox show` lists both. macOS settings are unchanged, and `moat uninstall` removes the rules.
- The default policy denies agent writes to `~/.cursor/sandbox.json` and a project's `.cursor/sandbox.json` (`kernel-self`, #324): a project file replaces Cursor's read boundary and adds hosts to its sandbox.
- Glob characters in a path operand no longer get past deny rules (#355). The shell expands an unquoted `*`, `?` or `[` before the command runs, but the operand was checked only as the literal word, so `cat .en?`, `cat .e*`, `cat .[e]nv` and `head -c 99 .en[v]` were allowed and `cat ~/.ss?/id_rsa` only asked while `cat .env` was denied. Such an operand, and a redirection target, is now checked as written and as every path it names in the real directories, resolved through symlinks, with `bash` matching rules (`*` skips dotfiles unless the pattern starts with `.`; an unmatched pattern is only its literal word). Quoted and escaped patterns stay literal, and directories are listed only for glob operands. A call whose globs name more than 256 paths, list more than 1024 directories or descend more than 8 levels of `**` asks (`unparseable`), and a deny among the named paths still wins. On hook-only setups (the Cursor editor, Claude Code on native Windows) this read secrets; the Standard tier's sandbox already blocked it.
- Brace expansion no longer gets past deny rules (#362). Bash makes several words of one with unquoted braces before anything else, so `cat .{env,x}` runs `cat .env .x`, but the word was checked as written and allowed; `cat .e{n,m}v`, `cat ~/.ssh/{id_rsa,x}` and `{cat,.env}` got through the same way. Words are now expanded as `bash` does it (comma lists, nested braces, `{1..3}`, `{a..e..2}`, `{01..10}`, several groups in one word, the command name included) and every word made is checked, and globbed when it holds `* ? [`. Quoted and escaped braces, `{}`, `{x}` and `${…}` stay literal. A word whose braces make more than 256 words asks (`unparseable`), and a deny elsewhere in the call still wins. `moat allow --always` of such a command writes the words it makes (`{cat,.env}` → `cat .env`), which is what the rule is matched against.

## [0.1.0-alpha.6] - 2026-10-08

### Added
- MoatBench measures prompts on real development work (#336): `tests/moatbench/workflows.yaml` runs step-by-step Rust, Node, Python, Go, git, Docker and Make workflows, and project-file edits, through Claude Code, Codex and Cursor payloads. Each step expects `allow`, or `ask` where the default policy means to (new dependencies, `git push`, `docker build`), and the scorecard prints the asks per workflow (`workflows: 152 steps, 26 asks (expected 17), 0 unexpected, 9 expected failures`). A policy change that adds an ask or deny to one of these steps fails CI. Four known false positives of the default policy are recorded as expected failures: `python3 -m venv .venv`, `source .venv/bin/activate`, `git rebase main` and a bare `make` ask. Steps can now be file edits (`edit:`, sent as Claude Code `Edit`, a Codex `apply_patch` `Update File`, and Cursor `Write`) and can carry their own `gap` marker.
- docs/EVIDENCE.md: what the OS layer does to hostile project scripts that the hook allows (#335). The differential suite runs each payload as `npm test` under `moat run`, in a throwaway home with fake secrets, and asserts the outcome on every pull request: on macOS (Seatbelt) and Linux (Landlock and seccomp), reading `~/.ssh/id_rsa` or `~/.aws/credentials`, writing outside the project, a direct TCP connection, a DNS query over UDP, a symlink from the project into `~/.ssh`, and editing the policy or Claude Code's settings fail with `EPERM` or `EACCES`, and an unlisted host gets the proxy's 403. On Linux a project `.env` stays readable, shown as a known gap. The Codex and Claude Code sandboxes are not run in CI and are listed as not verified. Regenerate with `MOAT_UPDATE_EVIDENCE=1 cargo test -p openmoat --test e2e differential`.
- `moat status` and `moat doctor` print one protection level per agent, derived only from the hook and sandbox checks they already make (#334): `hook + OS sandbox` (hook installed and current, generated sandbox in place and matching the policy), `hook only` (the Cursor editor, Claude Code on native Windows, or a sandbox that is missing, weakened or out of date, with the reason), or `not protected` (hook missing, out of date or unreadable), followed by one line of what that agent's hook and sandbox do not cover. `moat status --format json` prints the same per agent. The `moat` home screen names each protected agent's level (`Protecting Claude Code (hook + OS sandbox)`). The level never changes whether `status` or `doctor` reports a problem.

### Changed
- THREAT_MODEL no longer lists Claude Code `SendFile` as ungoverned: the hook checks its files as reads.
- Default policy: four steps of everyday development work that asked only through the catch-all default are now allowed by `dev-shell` (#350), and the MoatBench workflow suite has no known false positives left (`workflows: 152 steps, 19 asks (expected 19), 0 unexpected, 0 expected failures`). `python -m venv DIR` and `python3 -m venv DIR`: each directory is now an `fs.write`, a plain name included, so a venv outside the project, in `.git` or over `~/.moat` asks or is denied; `--clear` and `--upgrade`/`--upgrade-deps` ask. `source .venv/bin/activate` and `. .venv/bin/activate` (also `venv/`), spelled exactly and with no arguments; any other file, a path with `..`, or the same spelling after a `cd` out of the project asks. `git rebase <ref>`, like `git pull --rebase`; `--exec`/`-x`, `--interactive`/`-i`, `--edit-todo`, `--continue`, `--skip` and `--strategy`/`-s` ask, in any prefix git accepts and inside short-option clusters. A bare `make`, like `make test`; `make -f`, `make -C` and the existing recipe-shell overrides still ask. Stricter: `npm exec` and `npm x` now ask (`installs`) like `npx`, since both download a package they cannot find.
- README, the CLI crate README and docs/INSTALL.md no longer say every action is checked: they say OpenMoat checks the commands, file access, web requests and MCP calls the agents report through their hooks, which is what README Limits and THREAT_MODEL describe (#331).
- A permanent approval (`moat allow --always`, `moat allow --last --always`, or `a` in `moat`) prints what the rule will match before writing it (#333). For a shell command: `will allow: npm test (and the same command with extra arguments); deny rules still win`, since a command rule matches the approved command as a prefix. For files it says the rule names exactly those paths. The undo line is unchanged, and so is what a rule matches.
- The native Windows note for Claude Code now says `the hook still applies the policy` instead of `the hook still checks every call`, since some tools are not hooked (#331).
- `moat doctor`'s Cursor note is now its protection line, `hook only`, with the same reason: no OS sandbox from OpenMoat, and `moat run` covers the Cursor CLI.

### Security
- A part of a call the engine cannot classify no longer hides the decisions of the other parts (#345). It is one more `ask` (rule `unparseable`) merged with them, strictest wins, so a `deny` stands: an MCP call that reads `~/.ssh/id_rsa` and adds a `url` with no host, `cd "$X" && cat notes ~/.ssh/id_rsa`, `bash --frobnicate -c x; cat ~/.ssh/id_rsa` and `echo $(bash --frobnicate -c x) $(cat ~/.ssh/id_rsa)` were `ask` and are now denied by `secrets-paths`, and `bash -c "eval eval eval eval eval eval true"` by `pipe-to-shell`. The shell classifier keeps classifying the other simple commands after one it cannot, and the other paths of a command after one in an unknown directory. A call whose unclassifiable part is all it does still asks. Under a policy whose default is `deny`, a classified part that no rule allows is now denied even when another part cannot be classified. Session taint also counts the classified parts of such a call.
- `moat guard` denies (exit 2) when it has not decided within 10 seconds, instead of waiting for the host's hook timeout, after which Claude Code, Codex and the Continue CLI run the call (#337). The deny is not recorded, since what hangs may be the audit log. docs/THREAT_MODEL.md §5 has a table, with sources, of what each host does when the hook binary is missing, crashes, times out or prints bad output, and what `guard` answers for a malformed payload, an unknown event and a tool OpenMoat does not know; end-to-end tests cover OpenMoat's side for each host's payload shape, and that `moat status` and `moat doctor` report a hook whose binary is gone as `not protected`. The `known gaps` lines now say Claude Code and Codex also run the call when the hook crashes, and that Cursor blocks it.
- The audit log masks the exact values of the policy's brokered secrets (`secrets:`), whatever their format, before an event is stored, so they reach neither `audit.db` nor `moat audit export` (#338). Previously only known token formats were redacted, and a value sent as part of a host name was recorded by `moat proxy`. Matching ignores letter case and skips values shorter than 8 bytes, which occur in ordinary text. `moat guard` masks the values with a `file` or `env` source it can read in every call the policy decides; the proxy masks all of them. The working directory is now redacted too. Canary tests plant fake secrets in shell commands, paths, URLs, proxy headers and MCP arguments and check that none is stored and the hash chain still verifies.
- An MCP call whose arguments nest a path or URL deeper than 8 levels is now `ask` (rule `unparseable`, reason `mcp arguments not fully checked: nested deeper than 8 levels`) instead of being decided on the tool name alone (#332). The paths and URLs found above that depth are still checked, so a `deny` among them still wins. More than 1024 paths and URLs still denies the call.

## [0.1.0-alpha.5] - 2026-10-07

### Added
- docs/ADDING_AN_AGENT.md: how to add support for another AI coding agent, from mapping its hook payload to an action, through installing the hook and capturing fixtures, to what the pull request must include (#319). Linked from CONTRIBUTING.md and the README.
- docs/SANDBOX.md: how to run the Cursor CLI (`agent`) under `moat run` on macOS: its installation directory in `sandbox.read_roots`, an allow rule for `api2.cursor.sh`, `--write ~/.cursor`, `CURSOR_API_KEY` instead of the keychain, and `--sandbox disabled` for that run (#324).
- `moat doctor` notes, when the Cursor hook is installed, that OpenMoat gives Cursor no OS sandbox, so only the hook applies the policy, and that `moat run` covers the Cursor CLI (#324).

### Changed
- README, docs/INSTALL.md and docs/THREAT_MODEL.md no longer say that Cursor has no sandbox of its own. Cursor has one; `moat init` does not configure it, and Cursor can rerun a command outside it, so in the Cursor editor only the hook applies the policy (#324).

### Fixed
- On native Windows, `moat init` and `moat sandbox sync` no longer write Claude Code's `sandbox` block or `permissions.blockReadsOutsideWorkingDirectories` (#327). Claude Code's sandbox runs on macOS, Linux and WSL2 only, and with `failIfUnavailable: true` Claude Code exits at startup where it cannot run. `init`, `sandbox sync`, `sandbox show`, `doctor` and `status` now say `sandbox not available on native Windows; the hook still checks every call (use WSL2 for OS confinement)` for Claude Code instead of reporting the sandbox missing or weakened. Run `moat init` or `moat sandbox sync` again to remove those settings from an earlier version (they keep your other settings and re-pin the file). Codex is unchanged.

## [0.1.0-alpha.4] - 2026-10-07

### Changed
- README opens with a 40-second recording of the launch demo (`docs/assets/demo.gif`, from real `moat guard` verdicts). The demo script shortens the throwaway paths and the binary path in its output, and docs/DEMO.md no longer says the operating system does not enforce decisions.
- docs/INSTALL.md says how to upgrade: `brew update && brew upgrade moat`, since `brew upgrade` alone may not see a new release of the tap (#314).
- After a session grant (`o` in `moat`, or `moat allow --last` without `--always`), `moat` says that the grant covers only this agent session and that a new session asks again; for Claude Code, `claude --continue` resumes the session (#312).

### Fixed
- `moat init` no longer leaves out an agent silently when `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, `CODEX_HOME` or `CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR` names a directory that does not exist yet (#313). It prints one line per such agent with the variable and the directory, and says to start the agent once to create it and then run `moat init` again. It does not create the directory.

## [0.1.0-alpha.3] - 2026-10-07

### Added
- Policy key `taint.protected_writes` (#215): path globs that a write to asks once the session read untrusted content, in addition to the built-in list (CI, git hooks, build scripts, agent instructions). It extends the list and cannot shorten it; `moat policy lint` rejects `!` exclusions and globs that do not compile. Policies without the key behave as before.
- `moat allow --last` and `moat allow --last --always` approve file actions too, not only shell commands (#278). The last ask can be a file read, write or delete from a Claude Code file tool, a Codex `apply_patch` or a Cursor file tool. A session grant covers exactly those files for that action; `--always` adds an `fs.read` or `fs.write` rule for exactly those paths (as asked and with symlinks resolved) and prints it with its undo command. Deny rules still win. `moat` (the home screen) shows and approves the newest file ask the same way. `~/.moat/approvals.json` moves to version 2, which records the approved action; version 1 files are still read and are rewritten as version 2 on the next grant.

### Changed
- When Codex or a Cursor file tool cannot prompt, the deny message now says to run `moat` or `moat allow --last` (Cursor's said to add a policy rule and run `moat doctor --accept`). The Continue CLI message names `moat` too.

### Fixed
- `moat` says `1 decision` instead of `1 decisions` (#278).

## [0.1.0-alpha.2] - 2026-10-07

### Added
- `moat allow --site <host>`, `moat allow --dir <path>` and `moat allow --remove <id>` (#300). `--site` adds a `net` allow for one host name; it also covers web fetches. `--dir` adds an `fs.read` and `fs.write` allow for an existing directory and everything below it, stored as written and with its symlinks resolved. Both append to `~/.moat/policy.d/approved.yaml`, check that the merged policy lints, re-pin, and print the rule as written plus the `moat allow --remove` command that undoes it. Deny rules still win. Wildcards, URLs, the home directory, its ancestors and filesystem roots are refused. `--remove` takes out any `approved-N` rule, `--always` ones included, and `--always` now prints its undo command too. New attack fixtures show that an agent running `moat allow --site` or `--dir` is denied (`kernel-self`).
- `moat edit` (#300) opens `~/.moat/policy.yaml` in `$VISUAL`, `$EDITOR`, `vi` or (Windows) `notepad`, on a copy inside `~/.moat`. An unchanged copy changes nothing. A copy that does not lint is reported and never written, and you can reopen it. Otherwise it prints the lint warnings and a unified diff, and writes and re-pins only after `Apply? [y/N]` is answered `y`. The previous policy is kept in `~/.moat/policy.yaml.bak`. It needs a terminal and an intact lock, like `moat allow`.
- `moat uninstall [--hosts …] [--purge]` removes OpenMoat's hooks and sandbox settings from the agents and prints what it did per file (#282). A file unchanged since `moat init` gets its backup back byte for byte, a file `init` created is deleted, and a file changed since keeps those changes. The lock stops pinning the undone files; `~/.moat` stays unless `--purge`. It runs only from a terminal.
- `moat init` records the config directory of each agent it sets up in `~/.moat/hosts.json`, which the lock pins (#298). `status`, `doctor`, `allow`, `sandbox sync` and `uninstall` use the recorded directories, so a custom `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, `CODEX_HOME` or `CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR` no longer has to be exported for every command. `moat doctor` reports a variable that names another directory than the record instead of following it; `moat init` with the variable set moves the record. `kernel-self` protects both the recorded directory and the one the variable names. Nothing changes for agents in their default directories.
- `moat` with no subcommand shows one health line (the agents whose hook is installed, today's decisions with the number denied and asked) and then what needs a person (#299). Lock drift is listed and `Accept these changes? [y/N]` runs `moat doctor --accept` on `y`. The newest shell `ask` from today that no grant or permanent rule covers is shown with its rule and asks `Allow? [o]nce for this session / [a]lways / [n]o`, which runs `moat allow` for exactly that command. Otherwise it prints `Nothing needs you.` Without a terminal it prints the command to run and changes nothing.

### Changed
- `moat init` asks before changing an agent (#282). At a terminal it lists the agents it found with the files it would change and asks `Protect Claude Code (…)? [Y/n]` for each, then names the backups and `Undo anytime: moat uninstall`. `--hosts` and the new `--yes` skip the questions. Without a terminal and without either flag, `init` sets up `~/.moat` but changes no agent's files, and says how to go on: scripts that ran `moat init` need `--yes`.

### Fixed
- `moat status` and `moat doctor` show an agent that `moat init` left out (the person said no, or named other `--hosts`) as `· not set up` instead of a missing hook and five sandbox problems. Lock drift on a Claude Code settings file no longer names `theme`, which is not pinned (#287).

### Security
- `kernel-self` denies any `moat` command run under a pseudo-terminal wrapper (`script`, `unbuffer`, `tmux`, `screen`, quoted for `expect`, Python `pty.spawn`, `osascript`), so an agent cannot answer the questions of a bare `moat` (#299). An agent running `moat` or `moat status` without a wrapper still gets `ask`, and without a terminal `moat` changes nothing.
- `kernel-self` denies `moat edit` to agents, by name, by absolute path and under every pseudo-terminal wrapper already listed for `moat allow` (#300).
- `kernel-self` denies agent runs of `moat uninstall`, including through pseudo-terminal wrappers, like `moat allow` (#282).

## [0.1.0-alpha.1] - 2026-10-07

### Added
- Lock drift on a JSON or TOML file names the top-level keys that changed since the pin, in `moat doctor`, in `moat doctor --accept` before it re-pins, and in `guard`'s `kernel-integrity` reasons: `settings.json was modified: changed hooks; added theme` (#288). The lock records a digest per top-level key for those files; a lock written earlier keeps the plain message until its next re-pin.

### Changed
- `moat doctor` and `moat run` print one line with the number of places a sandbox is stricter or wider than the policy instead of the whole list; `--verbose` prints the list (#289). Problems (a weakened setting, drift) still print in full, and `moat sandbox show` still prints everything.
- `moat show` labels its time column `time (UTC)` and `moat replay` adds `UTC` to each session's start time; both were already UTC but unlabelled (#290).

### Fixed
- The policy lock leaves the top-level `theme` key out of Claude Code's `settings.json` digest, so Claude Code writing `"theme"` on first run no longer denies every call with `kernel-integrity` (#287). Every other key stays pinned, a file that does not parse is drift, and a lock pinned before this change keeps verifying until the next re-pin.

### Security
- `kernel-self` covers the state and host directories where `MOAT_HOME`, `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, `CODEX_HOME` or `CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR` moved them (#286). A write to `$MOAT_HOME/policy.yaml` or `$CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR/settings.json` was asked (`default`) instead of denied; the lock caught the change only afterwards. Patterns naming `~/.moat`, `~/.claude`, `~/.codex` or `~/.cursor` now also match the moved directory, in the hook, `moat policy check`, `moat run` and the Standard-tier sandbox settings.

## [0.1.0-alpha.0] - 2026-10-07

### Added
- Standard tier (ADR-018, #168, #169): `moat init` configures Claude Code's and Codex's own sandboxes from the policy through the enforcement IR (ADR-019). Claude Code's user settings get the `sandbox` block (`enabled`, `failIfUnavailable: true`, `allowUnsandboxedCommands: false`, `excludedCommands: []`, absolute deny and allow lists, `network.allowedDomains` with `strictAllowlist`) and `permissions.blockReadsOutsideWorkingDirectories: true`; Codex's `config.toml` gets a `[permissions.moat]` profile, `default_permissions = "moat"` and `features.network_proxy = true`. Other keys and comments are kept; each file is backed up to `<file>.moat-sandbox-backup` first. `moat sandbox show` prints what the policy compiles to with every loss (stricter) and allowance (wider); `moat sandbox sync` rewrites and re-pins (a person at a terminal, refused over a drifted lock). `moat doctor` and `moat status` check both hosts and print the losses.
- Policy: the additive `sandbox: { read_roots: [...] }` section lists paths the host sandboxes may read although no allow rule covers them (toolchains, system and temp directories). Only OS layers use it; the hook still asks for those reads and deny rules win inside them. The default policy lists a cross-platform set; a policy without the section gets that list.
- Differential suite (ADR-019, #170): `tests/differential/scenarios.yaml` runs each attack, benign flow and public sandbox-escape replay against every enforcement point on the machine (the hook decision, and each host sandbox whose binary is present) and fails on a layer that disagrees with the recorded verdict. A missing host binary skips that layer visibly. `scripts/ci/differential.sh` runs the full suite.
- Differential suite: the Codex host-sandbox layer (`codex sandbox -P moat`) runs every scenario, with `npm`/`cargo` shims so the hook sees an allowed `npm test`/`cargo build` while the payload inside the script is what the sandbox must stop. It confirms Codex blocks every filesystem attack (secret reads, writes outside the project, shell-rc, `.git` hooks, settings-escape, symlink read-escape) and the documented narrower results (a `.env.example` read and `.git` writes, so `git status`/`git commit`, are denied). It surfaced a gap (#229): `codex sandbox` leaves direct network open, so the generated egress allowlist is enforced only by the proxy (ADR-020); the two egress rows are marked as a known gap pointing at #229, visible in the matrix.
- Differential suite: the Claude Code host-sandbox layer runs the real `claude -p --bare` against a fake Anthropic API (`tests/differential/fake_api.py`) with the settings `moat sandbox show` generates (no hook), confirming Claude Code's sandbox blocks every attack, direct egress to link-local metadata included. Benign project work cannot be verified headlessly (Claude Code establishes working directories interactively), so those rows are skipped with a visible notice pointing at #238.
- `Action::McpTool` carries the paths and hosts an adapter derived from the call's arguments (`reads`, `writes`, `hosts`); the engine turns them into `fs.read`, `fs.write` and `net` atoms, so an allowed MCP tool name can no longer read `~/.aws/credentials` or fetch an unlisted host. Conformance fixtures accept `mcp_tool: { name, reads, writes, hosts }`.
- Claude Code and Cursor adapters derive those paths and hosts from MCP `tool_input` by argument name (`path`, `paths`, `file_path`, `source`, `destination`, `url`, …); write-shaped tool names (`write_*`, `edit_*`, `move_*`, `delete_*`) and `destination`/`target` arguments produce `fs.write`.
- Policy `secrets:` (ADR-020, `docs/POLICY.md` §2.1): brokered secrets, each with one exact `host`, a `header` and a `source` (`file`, `env` or `keychain`). Ids, hosts, header names and sources are validated, framing headers (`Host`, `Content-Length`, …) are refused, and a secret opens no host by itself. `moat policy lint` warns when no allow rule names the host, or when no deny rule keeps a `file` or `env` source from the agent. The optional `plain_http: true` allows injection into clear-text HTTP; it is off by default, and lint warns when it is set for a host that is not loopback. The agent's placeholder is `moat-secret:<id>:placeholder`.
- `openmoat-proxy` leak blocking for brokered secrets (ADR-020). It refuses a request whose head, or plain-HTTP body, carries a secret's placeholder or value to a host other than that secret's own, and records it as `proxy-secret`. Inside a body the check spans read boundaries, and the chunk that completes the secret is not forwarded. Values are zeroed on drop (`zeroize`) and never formatted, so reasons and `Debug` name only the id and host. The check matches exact bytes; `docs/THREAT_MODEL.md` §5 lists what it does not stop.
- `openmoat-proxy` injection for brokered secrets over plain HTTP (ADR-020), only for secrets that set `plain_http: true`. Without it, the request is forwarded with the placeholder, and the audit row records that the secret was not injected. In a request to the secret's host, the placeholder in the secret's header becomes the value, and the header is added when the request has none. The rewritten head is zeroed after it is written. HTTPS is not decrypted, so it gets no injection yet.
- `moat proxy` brokers the policy's `secrets:`. It reads each `file`, `env` or `keychain` source at start-up (macOS `/usr/bin/security`, Linux `/usr/bin/secret-tool`; Windows keychains are not supported yet) and exits 64 when one cannot be read. It prints each secret's placeholder for the agent and never the value.
- MoatBench mini (`docs/MOATBENCH.md`, #132): end-to-end scenarios in `tests/moatbench/<category>.yaml` sent through the real `moat guard` as Claude Code, Codex and Cursor payloads. Each step's verdict is read back from the audit log and checked against the host's answer; a scorecard lists verdicts per category, false positives and known gaps (scenarios marked with their issue). Runs in the quality gate.
- Repository policy (ADR-022, #128): a project's `<project>/.moat/policy.yaml` makes the user policy stricter for every tool call in the project. Its `deny` groups join the user's deny rules.
